P008 · Noh et al. - 2024 - Regime Support and Gender Quotas in Autocracies

One Algeria survey experiment: eight-field main-text review and Appendix B field-by-field review. Arab Barometer and LAPOP analyses are recorded as observational context.

P008-E01

Four-arm online priming experiment in Algeria, February 2020.

Sample recruitment Reported

An online survey recruited Algerian adults through Facebook advertisements and obtained consent in Qualtrics during February 5–21, 2020. Age and gender recruitment quotas addressed demographic imbalances. The article reports 1,119 experiment completers and a main analysis sample of 911 attention-check passers; active-duty military respondents were removed.

Instead, we conducted our survey online, recruiting Algerians through Facebook advertisements. Facebook ads have become an increasingly common survey recruitment device, in both the United States and the developing world (e.g., Cassese et al. 2013; Guiler 2020; Samuels and Zucco 2014). Following this scholarship, we purchased advertisements on Facebook shown to all adult Algerians. 16 Clicking on the advertisement took users out of Facebook and into Qualtrics, where they first agreed to a consent form before viewing the survey.17 Between February 5 and 21, 2020, 1,119 Algerians clicked on the advertisement and completed the survey experiment (for more details on survey methodology, see Appendix B.1 of the Supplementary Material).

Main article · PDF page 8

We followed Zhang et al. (2018) in implementing age and gender quota sampling during recruitment, bringing our sample more in line with the population on observable demographics

Main article · PDF page 8

Separately, we also targeted one advertisement to likely military personnel, allowing us to oversample the military. We remove all active-duty personnel for the analysis in this article.

Main article · PDF page 8

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

Four respondent-level conditions shared factual information about the 2012 gender quota. Additional text emphasized gains for women, legitimacy gains for the regime, or both gains; the control received the common quota information. Table 1 gives the complete wording and analyzed arm sizes (236 control, 212 women, 224 regime, 239 trade-off).

The treatment groups then received additional text priming them to thinkabout how these quotas led to gains for women (Treatment 1), led to gains for the regime (Treatment 2), or led to gains for both, compelling them to evaluate the trade-off (Treatment 3).

P010 · Masterson - 2024 - Refugee Networks, Cooperation, and Resource Access

One linked two-level randomized study of meeting recruitment and sampled participants, with group and individual contrasts recorded separately within each field. Relevant publisher appendix sections 1–10 and 12 reviewed; the separate coding guide and supplementary regression-table files remain follow-up sources.

P010-E01

Linked group- and individual-level network experiment across 56 community meetings in Lebanon and Jordan, 2016.

Sample recruitment Reported

The study organized 56 single-gender meetings in 14 sites in Lebanon and Jordan in 2016. Initial participants were randomly sampled from UNHCR registration records; networked meetings recruited additional participants through referrals. There were 491 attendees, including 258 randomly sampled individuals used in the individual-level comparison.

I organized 56 community meetings with Syrian refugees across 14 cities, towns, and refugee camps in Lebanon and Jordan.

Main article · PDF page 6

The experimental procedures began with 56 individuals randomly sampled from the UN Refugee Agency registration records, who each served as the first participant for one of 56 community meetings. I randomly assigned half of the individuals to a group composed of their close network, recruited through referral sampling. I assigned the other half to a group composed of other refugees randomly sampled from the U.N. records in the same town.

Main article · PDF page 6

Two experienced moderators led 56 community meetings, comprising 491 individuals. The mean group size was 8.8 participants, ranging from 6 to 10 people. Attendance rates were balanced across treatment and control arms. Meetings were either all-male or allfemale, with no mixed-gender meetings.

Main article · PDF page 8

The 56 community meetings comprised a total of 491 individuals. 16 Two hundred and fifty-eight of these participants were randomly sampled, and therefore individual-level treatment assignment is exchangeable for these participants.

Main article · PDF page 8

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

Networked meetings were formed from a randomly sampled seed and referrals from close contacts; comparison meetings used independently sampled refugees from the same town. The intervention changes meeting composition and the setting experienced by randomly sampled participants. Both arms discuss common prerecorded public-goods vignettes.

At the group level, I formed networked (treated) groups through referral recruitment, where each group is a randomly sampled network neighborhood from the census. 10 I compare these groups to control groups composed of randomly sampled individuals, thereby defining the group-level comparison.

Main article · PDF page 6

Network neighborhood recruitment took place as follows. A single person was randomly sampled from the census, who then referred three close friends outside their family and household, who each in turn referred two or three friends outside their family and household.

Main article · PDF page 6

To study how participants engage in community problem solving, a trained moderator presented audio vignettes in each meeting describing problems that are common in refugee communities. The scenarios capture common collective problems that Syrian refugees face in Lebanon and Jordan pertaining to issues of public safety, freedom of movement, resource distribution, and the ability to earn a basic livelihood.

Main article · PDF page 7

Human review: pending

Outcomes Reported

Group and individual outcomes include reciprocal, problem-relevant dialogue comments and counts of unique accessible resource types mentioned in discussion. Disaggregated resource-access indicators cover leaders, brokers, sulha, host community, NGOs, government, and police. Transcripts were coded with a fixed guide and treatment-blind coding.

Outcomes include metrics of cooperation and resource diversity. To test hypotheses about the group-level network effect on collaboration (H1) and the individual-level network effect on collaboration (H3), I examine the number of comments where participants actively discussed responses to the public goods problems with other participants. Dialogue is coded as comments that met two conditions: (1) relevance, meaning they discussed responses to the community problem and (2) reciprocity, meaning they either responded to a previous comment or prompted a direct response from another participant.

Main article · PDF page 9

The resources discussed included Syrian leaders, brokers between the Syrian and the host community, traditional dispute resolution involving sheikhs ( sulha), the host community, NGOs, the government, and the police.

Main article · PDF page 9

The outside researchers and I were blind to treatment status during codebook development and tagging.

P012 · Goldfien et al. - 2023 - The Domestic Sources of International Reputation

Three independent samples: main, supplementary, and pilot conjoint studies. Main-text methods/results and appendix experimental sections reviewed. Postconjoint questionnaire randomization is documented within the associated study.

P012-E01

June 2021 Arcadia conjoint

Sample recruitment Reported

The June 2021 main study recruited 1,878 respondents through Lucid and retained respondents passing a pretreatment attention check.

We conducted our study in June 2021 ( N = 1,878).19 We recruited respondents through Lucid, an online survey platform. 20 Prior work has shown treatment effects using Lucid similar to those that occur with other commonly used online platforms (Coppock and McClellan 2019). To address concerns about inattentiveness among online respondents (Aronow et al. 2020), we retained only respondents who passed a pretreatment attention check.

Main article · PDF page 10

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

Five independently randomized binary leader attributes describe coup versus regular accession, protest repression versus restraint, firm versus compromising domestic bargaining, proceeding versus cancelling a controversial speech, and fighting versus concessions in a past crisis. Each respondent rates five single profiles in a fictional Arcadia–US dispute.

We used a fully randomized ratings-based conjoint design (Hainmueller, Hopkins, and Yamamoto 2014). We gave subjects five tasks. For each task, respondents were shown a profile of a leader ’sp a s td o m e s t i ca n d international choices and asked to rate the likelihood that t h el e a d e rw o u l ds t a n df i r mi nah y p o t h e t i c a l international crisis. Respondents viewed leader profiles detailing all four domestic choices discussed above as well as information on whether or not the leader fought in a past international crisis.

P027 · Kreft and Agerberg - 2024 - Imperfect Victims Civilian Men, Vulnerability, and Policy Preferences

Five independently recruited studies: three US studies and two UK replications. Main-text methods/results and supplementary sample tables, replication description, estimates, instruments, deviations and ethics reviewed.

P027-E01

US Syria information experiment

Sample recruitment Reported

The US Syria information study recruited 1,294 Prolific respondents and administered the survey through Qualtrics. Studies 1–2 used age/gender/race representative sampling; Study 3 used the larger available panel sample.

The survey experiments were administered through Qualtrics and carried out with U.S. respondents recruited through Prolific, an online platform shown to yield high data quality (Peer et al. 2022). Studies 1 and 2 used Prolific ’s representative sample option, whereas the larger sample size required precluded this option for Study 3. This means that the samples for the first two studies are representative of the U.S. population on age, gender, and race.

Estimand Reported

Average marginal component effects of profile attributes on refugee-group selection, plus the change in the majority-male profile effect induced by respondent-level information. A separate contrast estimates information effects on support for male-focused aid.

Main article · PDF page 11

To test H5b, we recoded the variable indicating the share of men refugees in a profile into a binary variable that equals 1 if a profile contains a majority of men refugees (75% or 100%) and 0 otherwise (0%, 25%, or 50%). We then interacted the binary majority men variable with the information treatment that was given randomly earlier in the survey to half of the respondents and re-estimated the main model for the conjoint experiment with the interaction included.

P044 · Amar et al. - 2025 - Countering Misinformation Early Evidence from a Classroom-Based Field Experiment in India

One village-randomized media-literacy trial with an active conversational-English control, student follow-ups and guardian spillover outcomes. Main design and relevant appendix sampling, allocation, curriculum, attendance, measurement, models and attrition sections reviewed.

P044-E01

Bihar media-literacy curriculum versus conversational-English instruction across 583 villages.

Sample recruitment Reported

13,592 baseline students in grades 8–12, aged 13–18, from 583 villages near 100 libraries in 32 Bihar districts. Households opted into a government-endorsed course before randomization. Recruitment used Jeevika household lists and interest visits.

In our study, participants were school students in grades 8 through 12, aged between 13 and 18 years old. To dispense the intervention classes, Jeevika made available to us 100 community libraries across 32 districts in Bihar.

Main article · PDF page 7

In each of the 583 selected villages, Jeevika provided household lists based on enrollment in state programs. From these, households with children in grades 8 –12 were identified as eligible to participate in our study. Jeevika staff visited these homes to ask whether the child would be interested in participating in a government education program, producing a final list of 20–25 interested households per village. Our survey team then conducted an in-person baseline survey before randomization. Crucially, we note that randomization occurred after students opted in, avoiding issues with differential opt-in rates between treatment and control. Everyone involved in the study —including teachers, implementation partners, government officials, and coauthors—were blind to treatment status during recruitment and baseline data collection.

Main article · PDF page 8

13,592 respondents across 583 villages, with 49.9% assigned to treatment and 50.1% to control.

Main article · PDF page 9

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

Four after-school media/information-literacy sessions combine critical thinking, source assessment, health misinformation examples and family discussion. Active controls attend four conversational-English sessions. Classes run November 2023–March 2024 with separately recruited teacher pools.

We ran our classes in these libraries from November 2023 to March 2024, delivering classes after school hours.

Main article · PDF page 7

Control group units participated in four modules of conversational English language classes, serving as a placebo rather than a pure control.

Main article · PDF page 9

We ultimately implemented a curriculum of four sessions on basic conversational English given students had very limited prior exposure. The curriculum focused on spoken skills, covering selfintroduction, naming objects, describing activities, and asking questions, using role-playing and group exercises similar to those in the treatment group

Main article · PDF page 9

The teacher selection and training differed between the treatment and control groups. DataLEADS recruited and trained treatment teachers, while English class teachers were recruited via a local Bihar consultant, resulting in variations in socioeconomic characteristics and teaching experiences.

Main article · PDF page 9

Human review: pending

Outcomes Reported

Seven first-endline outcome families cover accuracy, sharing, health attitudes, source trust, engagement attitudes/behaviors and awareness. ICW indices are standardized against controls. A later telephone endline measures students and guardians.

Building on these intuitions, we pre-specified and included seven distinct families of outcomes in the first endline survey: accuracy discernment, sharing discernment, health attitudes, trust in sources, engagement with misinformation countermeasures (attitudes), engagement with misinformation countermeasures (behaviors), and awareness of misinformation. 14 Each outcome family comprises multiple survey items. For the analysis, we construct inverse-covariance weighted (ICW) indices that aggregate and weight these items, standardized relative to the control-group mean and SD. Our primary analyses focus on these seven indices.

Main article · PDF page 10

In the second endline, we measured accuracy discernment for both the respondent and one parent or guardian.

Treatment assignment Partially reported

Villages are assigned approximately equally within library area and spillover stratum, after recruitment and baseline. Spillover strata reflect whether multiple sampled villages share a Gram Panchayat.

Before randomization, we categorized each village as having either high or low spillover potential, based on how many sampled villages fell within the same Gram Panchayat (GP) —al o c a l administrative unit encompassing multiple villages. Spillovers were expected to be higher within the same GP, as children from those villages are more likely to attend the same schools or classes. Therefore, GPs with multiple selected villages were classified as high-spillover, while those with only one selected village were considered lowspillover. We then randomly assigned to treatment and control within each library area and spillover stratum (see Supplementary Section A for details).

Main article · PDF page 8

Crucially, we note that randomization occurred after students opted in, avoiding issues with differential opt-in rates between treatment and control.

Main article · PDF page 8

Due to the possibility of non-compliance, our main specification estimates the intent-to-treat ITTY effect: the effect of being assigned to the treatment group.

Main article · PDF page 10

To complement the ITT analysis, we also estimate complier average causal effects (CACE).15

P045 · Badrinathan - 2021 - Educative Interventions to Combat Misinformation Evidence from a Field Experiment in India

Eight-field main-text review with completed field-by-field checks of relevant supplementary sections. Open questions and human validation are recorded separately.

P045-E01

One blocked, three-arm field experiment; subgroup analyses and story-order randomization are components, not independent experiments.

Sample recruitment Reported

In Gaya, Bihar, India, polling booths were randomly sampled as survey areas and households selected by random walk. Eligibility required a personal phone and access to the internet and WhatsApp over the preceding six months. One eligible adult was randomly selected within households with multiple eligible members. Of eligible respondents, 94.5% consented. There were 1,309 baseline intervention recipients and 1,224 successful follow-ups.

The sample was drawn from the city of Gaya in the state of Bihar in India.

Main article · PDF page 6

Respondents were selected through a random walk procedure. Within the sampling area, a random sample of polling booths (smallest administrative units) were selected to serve as enumeration areas. Within each enumeration area, enumerators were instructed to survey 10 –12 households following a random walk procedure.

Main article · PDF page 6

Once a household was selected, respondents could qualify for the study based on three preconditions designed to maximize familiarity with the Internet: respondents were required to have their own cellphone (i.e., not a shared household phone), working Internet for six months prior to the survey, and WhatsApp downloaded on the phone. If multiple members of a household qualified based on the preconditions, a randomly selected adult member was requested to participate in the study.

Main article · PDF page 7

Despite this, the study had a high response rate: of all those who were eligible for the study, 94.5% agreed to participate.

Main article · PDF page 7

During the baseline survey, 1,309 respondents were administered the intervention. The enumerators successfully located 1,224 of these respondents, resulting in an attrition rate of 6.5%.

Main article · PDF page 7

Record area sampling, within-household selection, and treatment assignment separately. The baseline count is 1,309 and the follow-up count is 1,224.

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

Both media-literacy arms received instruction and fact-checking-tool training, correcting either four pro-BJP or four anti-BJP false stories. The placebo condition received instruction on plastic pollution and a leaflet on reducing plastic use.

For this study, the respondents were randomized into one of three groups, two treatment and one placebo control. Table 1 summarizes the three groups. Respondents in both treatment groups received the pedagogical intervention. However, one group received corrections to four pro-BJP false stories and the other received corrections to four anti-BJP false stories. Besides differences in the stories that were factchecked, the tips on the flyer remained the same for both treatment groups. Respondents in the placebo control group received a symmetric treatment where enumerators spoke about plastic pollution and were given a flyer on tips to reduce plastic usage.

Main article · PDF page 6

Human review: pending

The key outcome of interest is whether the intervention positively affected respondents ’ ability to identify misinformation. To this end, respondents were shown a series of 14 news stories.

Main article · PDF page 7

Twelve were false and two were true.

The dependent variable MisinformationId counts the number of stories accurately identified as true or false.

Treatment assignment Reported

Two blocks based on BJP versus other party identification; respondents are randomly assigned within blocks to either treatment arm or placebo. The main text reports one-third of the overall sample in each arm.

To control for potential imbalance in the sample, a randomized block design was used. The respondents who identified with the BJP were one block, and those who identified with any other party were another block. Within each block, the respondents were randomly assigned to one of the three experimental groups described in Table 1 . This design ensured that each treatment condition had an equal proportion of BJP and non-BJP partisans. Overall, the sample was equally divided between the two treatment and placebo control groups (i.e., one third of the sample in each of the three groups).

Main article · PDF page 6

Classified as stratified random assignment. This main-text passage does not describe implementation details such as the random-number tool.

Beyond the average treatment effect, I expect treatment effects to differ conditional on a single factor previously identified in the literature as a significant predictor of information consumption: partisan identity.

Treatments Reported

A 2 × 2 design crosses actor skin tone (light/dark) with spoken language (English/Spanish). Respondents watch a 30-second video conveying the same hurricane-relief message. Both language versions have English subtitles. Two same-age female actors were matched on clothing, accent, and English fluency.

Survey respondents were randomly assigned to watch one of four 30-second videos that explain the needs of Hurricane Maria victims in Puerto Rico. In this 2 /C2 2 factorial design, I define treatment conditions on two dimensions: skin tone and language. Both treatments are binary contrasts, which (as is particularly relevant for the skin tone treatment) do not account for finer gradations that often serve as markers of social difference. Table 1 shows the four treatment conditions.

The residualization model includes age, female, education, white, southern residence, Republican, Trump vote, income, citizenship knowledge, and perceived language. These are described as baseline covariates. Footnote 11 describes selecting covariate combinations using control outcomes and adjusted R-squared.

I first rescale the observed potential outcomes as a linear function of a set of baseline covariates. This method reduces variance of potential outcomes that is uninformative about the causal effect and thereby increases the power to detect effects (Rosenbaum 2002).

I selected these pretreatment covariates for residualization by, first, regressing only control outcomes on combinations of the available covariates in the data and, second, choosing the combinations of covariates with the highest adjusted R 2. Ugandan political-oversight intervention across 260 subcounties, including the challenger extension.

Sample recruitment Reported

The study selected 28 Ugandan districts based on budget reporting, regional coverage, and absence of other accountability interventions, and included 260 rural subcounties. The panel comprises 2,365 politicians and 461 bureaucrats. Town councils, difficult-to-reach islands, and enumerator-training locations were excluded.

The sample consists of 2,365 elected politicians and 461 bureaucrats in 260 subcounties in 28 districts.

P067 · Kalla and Broockman - 2022 - “Outside Lobbying” over the Airwaves A Randomized Field Experiment on Televised Issue Ads

Eight-field main-text review with completed field-by-field checks of relevant supplementary sections. Open questions and human validation are recorded separately.

P067-E01

One field experiment with three ad arms and randomized follow-up timing; outcomes/comparisons are kept within the same experiment.

Sample recruitment Reported

Registered voters using a particular television provider in six US states received mailed invitations to an online baseline survey. Of 1,082,605 invitees, 32,923 responded. The main text reports excluding the 1,501 most supportive respondents and a randomized sample of 31,404.

The experiment began by recruiting registered voters ( n=1,082,605) via mail for an ostensibly unrelated online baseline survey, presented as the first in a series of surveys about political and social topics. These registered voters all subscribed to particular TV providers with the technological ability to target television advertising at the household level. The experiment occurred in California, Colorado, Michigan, North Carolina, Tennessee, and Wisconsin.

Main article · PDF page 2

A total of 32,923 voters responded to this baseline survey, during which we gathered pretreatment covariates for improved prec ision and respondents ’ email addresses to invite them to an endline survey. From this survey, we constructed an index of respondents’ baseline opinions related to immigration, LGBTQ issues, and partisan political views. Due to budget constraints, at this stage, we removed the 1,501 individuals who were already in the most supportive deciles of both the immigration and LGBTQ indices.

Main article · PDF page 2

We randomized 31,404 voters to three weeks of interest group ads about either immigration or transgender nondiscrimination.

Main article · PDF page 1

Sample counts require reconciliation: 32,923 minus 1,501 equals 31,422, which exceeds the reported randomized sample of 31,404 by 18. The source of this discrepancy remains unresolved.

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

Three household-level advertising arms: Eddie plus Prosperous Future; MariCruz/Kandy plus Prosperous Future; and the LGBTQ-themed Open to All. Immigration-ad effects use the LGBTQ arm as comparison; LGBTQ-ad effects use the pooled immigration arms. All arms receive advertising.

We next block-randomly assigned baseline survey respondents at the household level to one of three conditions: (1) a group that received two immigration ads ( “Eddie” and “Prosperous Future ”; n=10,467), (2) a group that received two immigration ads ( “MariCruz/Kandy” and the same “Prosperous Future ” ad; n=10,468), and (3) a group that received an ad about LGBTQ rights ( “Open to All ”; n=10,469).

Main article · PDF page 3

To estimate the effect of the two immigration ad conditions, we use the LGBTQ ad condition as the comparison group.

Main article · PDF page 4

All three arms receive ads. The immigration-ad comparison group receives LGBTQ-themed advertising.

Human review: pending

Outcomes Partially reported

Measures cover immigration and LGBTQ prejudice and policy support, forming four component indices and two overall indices. Additional measures include willingness to take political action, ad recall, and knowledge of immigrants' tax payments. Follow-up invitations are sent during advertising, one day after, or three days after the campaign.

To measure these constructs, the posttreatment survey included multiple items measuring immigration prejudice (eight items), immigration policy (three items), LGBTQ prejudice (four items), and LGBTQ policy (six items). As we preregistered, we combine these items into four indices as well as two overall LGBTQ and immigration indices containing all 11 immigration and 10 LGBTQ items. Following our preanalysis plan, we formed these indices by taking the first dimension from a factor analysis of the appropriate items and recoded all indices such that positive values indicate the intended effect of greater public support. We rescale all indices to have mean 0 and standard deviation 1.

Main article · PDF page 3

In addition to these indices, we also asked respondents how likely they would be to take various political actions (e.g., contact your Congressperson) about each issue on a 1 (not at all likely)t o5( extremely likely) scale.

Main article · PDF page 3

We also asked two items as manipulation checks to assess whether the advertisements were successfully delivered and memorable. We asked whether respondents recalled seeing advertisements on television about several topics, with separate items for immigration and LGBTQ advertisements. We refer to these as measures of advertisement recall (see Figure OA1 for wording).

Main article · PDF page 3

We also asked about factual knowledge of the immigration system. The “MariCruz/Kandy” immigration advertisement mentioned that undocumented workers pay taxes several times. To measure whether respondents learned this, we presented a list of potentially true facts and asked respondents which they believed to be completely true. One statement was about undocumented immigrants paying taxes.

P075 · Blair et al. - 2022 - Preventing Rebel Resurgence after Civil War A Field Experiment in Security and Justice Provision in

Community-randomized ComunPaz trial plus embedded list and endorsement measurement experiments; main text and relevant Appendix A design, sampling, compliance and estimator sections reviewed. Secondary Appendix S is identified for follow-up.

P075-E01

ComunPaz community-randomized program.

Sample recruitment Reported

149 communities in 24 purposively selected municipalities across four Colombian regions with historical FARC presence. Eligible populated centers have fewer than 5,000 residents. Endline targets 18 randomly selected adult residents and eight JAC leaders per community, plus municipal police and Police Inspectors.

Our sample consists of 149 communities distributed across 24 municipalities in four purposefully selected regions of Colombia: Ariari-Guayabero, Oriente Antioqueño, Nordeste Antioqueño, and Centro del Valle del Cauca. 2 Our primary site selection criterion was historical FARC presence.

Main article · PDF page 6

Within selected municipalities we sampled all centros poblados (“populated centers ”) with fewer than 5,000 residents. We discarded populated centers with more than 5,000 residents in order to reduce heterogeneity in cluster size and because the intervention was intentionally designed to target relatively small communities.

Main article · PDF page 6

We surveyed 18 randomly selected residents and eight purposively selected leaders in each community in our sample. The residents survey included an endorsement experiment designed to measure support for the police, JACs, and armed groups and a list experiment designed to measure reliance on armed groups to resolve disputes. We also surveyed one police commander and one Police Inspector in each municipality.

Main article · PDF page 10

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

ComunPaz comprises four modules addressing legal responsibilities, trust and coordination among police, Police Inspectors, JACs and residents, culminating in a community-specific dispute-resolution route. Delivery ran October 2018–May 2019.

Module 1 targets police officers and Police Inspectors; Module 2 targets JACs. These modules help participants understand the legal division of labor between state and communal authorities, identify the comparative advantages of state and communal approaches to dispute resolution, and locate the most important sources of conflict in the communities under their jurisdictions. Module 3 targets police officers, Police Inspectors, and JACs, and attempts to build trust while helping participants develop concrete, actionable proposals for collaborating to resolve disputes like those identified in Modules 1 and 2. Module 4 targets police officers, Police Inspectors, JACs, and citizens and, again, attempts to build trust while disseminating information about the proposals developed in Module 3. The program culminates with the promulgation of a ruta de atención (“response route”) for resolving disputes, as in Figure 1. Response routes are tailored to each community and then displayed prominently in a central location in each village.

Main article · PDF page 6

The program was implemented between October 2018 and May 2019 by the UN Development Programme (UNDP), the Colombian government ’s National Planning Department (DNP), and the Conflict Analysis Resource Center (CERAC), a Bogotá think tank.

Main article · PDF page 6

Human review: pending

Outcomes Reported

Outcomes include unresolved/violent disputes, reliance on armed/state/communal authorities, information, trust and coordination. Survey outcomes are measured about seven months after delivery; petition returns and WhatsApp-group formation are collected one week after survey.

Following our theoretical framework, in our PAP we hypothesized that ComunPaz would reduce the prevalence of (H1) unresolved and (H2) violent disputes and decrease (H3) reliance on armed groups while increasing reliance on (H4) JACs and (H5) police officers and Police Inspectors.

Main article · PDF page 8

First, we conducted an endline survey roughly seven months after the end of the intervention.

Main article · PDF page 10

Second, we administered two costly behavioral measures. The first was designed to operationalize demand for coordination between police officers, Police Inspectors, and JACs. At the end of the residents survey, respondents were given a petition requesting additional involvement of municipal authorities in local dispute resolution. Residents were instructed to deliver the signed petition to the JAC president within seven days of the survey. We then called each JAC president to ask how many petitions they received. The second behavioral measure sought to operationalize actual coordination between police officers, Police Inspectors, and JACs. At the end of the leaders survey, respondents were encouraged to create a WhatsApp group to facilitate coordination with municipal authorities, and they were also given the name and phone number of the police commander and Police Inspector in their jurisdiction. We then called each JAC president a week later to ask whether they had created a WhatsApp group.

Main article · PDF page 10

Human review: pending

Pretreatment covariates Partially reported

Individual controls (age, gender, household size, education, employment, wall/floor quality) are measured at endline and justified by the authors as stable. Community controls use administrative population and distances. Historical exposure and governance measures support heterogeneity analyses.

We include individual-level controls for age, gender, household size, educational attainment, employment status, and two proxies for socioeconomic status (quality of walls and floors). These controls were measured in the endline survey; although they are posttreatment, they either cannot (e.g., age) or are very unlikely (e.g., educational attainment) to be affected by treatment assignment. We also include communitylevel controls for population, distance to the nearest arterial road, and distance to the departmental capital, measured using administrative data from the Instituto Agustín Codazzi and the planning office of each municipality.

Main article · PDF page 11

Main-text details requiring clarification:

  • Collection dates for administrative covariates and timing of recalled moderators need clarification.

Human review: pending

Treatment assignment Reported

Community-level assignment is stratified by region and blocked by population. Four communities per block receive treatment in the two Antioquia regions and two elsewhere: 72 treated of 149. Municipal implementation order is also randomized.

Treatment was randomly assigned at the community level. We stratified by region and blocked by population to mitigate the bias that can arise when average cluster size varies between the treatment and control groups (Imai, King, and Nall2009). We then randomly assigned four communities to treatment in each block in Oriente Antioqueño and Nordeste Antioqueño and two communities in each block in Ariari-Guayabero and Centro del Valle del Cauca, for a total of 72 treatment communities. We also randomized the order in which municipalities would be treated.

Main article · PDF page 8

Human review: pending

Estimand Reported

The primary estimand is the ITT of assignment to ComunPaz. Effects are reported separately for resident, leader and community-level outcomes. Average effect sizes express related outcomes in control-group standard deviations. CACE sensitivity reclassifies partial compliers.

We estimate the intention-to-treat effect (ITT) on all outcomes. Dependent variables from the residents and leaders surveys are operationalized at the individual level. Dependent variables from the police and Police Inspectors surveys are operationalized at the community level. Our two behavioral measures are operationalized at the community level as well.

Main article · PDF page 11

AES coefficients are interpreted in terms of standard deviations from the control group mean.

Main article · PDF page 11

As a robustness check, we computed (approximate) upper and lower bounds on the complier average causal effect (CACE) by first classifying partial noncompliers as compliers and estimating the CACE, then reclassifying them as noncompliers and reestimating the CACE.

Main article · PDF page 12

Human review: pending

Analysis model Reported

AES uses a jointly estimated seemingly unrelated regression framework; single outcomes use OLS. Models include block effects and inverse realized-assignment-probability weights. Individual outcomes cluster standard errors by community; community outcomes omit individual controls and clustering.

For individual-level outcomes, we cluster our standard errors at the community level. For community-level outcomes, we omit individual-level controls and do not cluster our standard errors. Because the probability of treatment assignment varied across blocks, we weight each community by the inverse of the probability of assignment to its realized treatment status. The probability of assignment to treatment ranged from one-third to two-thirds, implying that no community received a weight more than twice that of any other. All specifications include block fixed effects. We report results with multiple comparisons corrections in Appendix A.5.2.

Main article · PDF page 11

The πj are jointly estimated in a seemingly unrelated regression framework. The J dependent variables are stacked to compute a variance-covariance matrix for testing the statistical significance of τ, the AES.

Main article · PDF page 11

Human review: pending

Implementation and responses Partially reported

Three treated communities receive no program; other communities receive partial modules. All communities enter the ITT endline. Behavioral follow-up is missing for 32/149 communities (19.4% treated,23.4% control); Table 7 uses 117 communities. Main resident/leader analyses use 2,673/1,182 observations.

Fortunately, there are only three treatment communities that we can classify as “full” noncompliers, in that they were not treated at all. Other communities are more appropriately classified as “partial” noncompliers, as they received some (but not all) modules of the intervention. We administered the endline survey in all communities and include both full and partial noncompliers in the treatment group for purposes of our ITT analyses.

Main article · PDF page 12

These measures suffer from missing data. When we attempted to contact JAC leaders a week after the survey was complete, many were unreachable. As a result, we are missing data on petitions and WhatsApp groups for 32 of the 149 communities in our sample.

Main article · PDF page 17

We are missing data on petitions and WhatsApp groups for 19.4% of treatment communities and 23.4% of control communities. These proportions are not statistically different from each other.

P091 · Larson and Lewis - 2025 - Reducing Prejudice toward Refugees Evidence That Social Networks Influence Attitude Change in Ugand

One household-randomized study in four Ugandan villages; main-text design and analyses checked alongside supplementary intervention, ATE, attrition, network and timing sections.

P091-E01

Perspective-taking conversations and network spillovers in four Ugandan villages.

Sample recruitment Reported

Four purposively selected West Nile villages contained 521 participating households at baseline and 488 at endline. Selection considered village size, urban and refugee-settlement distance, geographic/religious variation and leadership consent.

We selected four villages from the population of villages in West Nile using three criteria: size of village (aiming for an average-sized village of roughly 100 –150 households), distance of at least 10 kilometers from the nearest peri-urban or urban area (to help ensure similarity of average wealth and education, as well as relevance of word-of-mouth networks), and distance of between 40 and 60 minutes via public transit to the nearest refugee settlement (to help ensure similarity of contact frequency with refugees). 13 We also sought geographic diversity within West Nile (hence our villages come from three districts) and variation in village-level social heterogeneity (religion); allowing for this variation enables us to probe whether either factor strongly obstructs spillover.

Main article · PDF page 6

Baseline hhs 127 98 146 150 521 Endline hhs 116 85 142 145 488

Main article · PDF page 7

Human review: pending

Treatments Reported

A 10–15 minute nonjudgmental conversation exchanges refugee narratives, encourages perspective taking and addresses concerns. Controls receive the survey. Follow-up occurs approximately two weeks later.

Our intervention was a brief (roughly 10–15 minutes) conversation in which the visitor nonjudgmentally exchanges narratives about refugees with the individual and encourages them to take the perspective of refugees.

Main article · PDF page 5

The remaining control households were only surveyed. Treatment and control households were surveyed again approximately 2 weeks later.

Main article · PDF page 5

Human review: pending

Outcomes Reported

The primary pro-refugee index sums six five-point items, oriented toward warmer attitudes, ranging from 6 to 30. The study analyzes immediate and 2–3 week changes, network-neighbor similarity and endline attitudes.

Our primary dependent variable is an index of attitudes toward refugees that aggregates responses to six survey questions. The questions were designed to replicate survey instruments in Hopkins, Sides and Citrin (2019) and Kalla and Broockman ( 2020), lightly modified to suit the Ugandan refugee context.

Main article · PDF page 7

Our analyses use an index constructed from the sum of the rescaled responses to these six questions as the dependent variable which ranges from 6 (the least prorefugee answer to all six questions was selected) to 30 (the most pro-refugee answer to all six questions was selected). We refer to this index as the pro-refugee score, with higher values indicating warmer attitudes, and lower values colder ones.

Main article · PDF page 8

The short-term change is the difference between the treated respondents ’ pro-refugee score at the end of the baseline survey (after treatment) and their score measured earlier in that survey (before treatment). Long-term change is the difference between the treated respondents ’ score in the endline survey (2 –3 weeks after treatment) and their initial baseline score.

Main article · PDF page 9

Human review: pending

Pretreatment covariates Reported

The regression adjusts for own baseline attitudes and baseline network-neighbor attitudes and degree. Network measures were collected before treatment. Distance-to-persuaded/backlash variables use immediate responses to treatment.

To test this logic, we count how many neighbors a respondent has (# Neighbs), indicate whether any were treated (Treated Neighbs), compute the average baseline score of these neighbors (Neighbs Bl Atts), and account for the respondent ’s own baseline attitudes (Baseline Atts).

Main article · PDF page 13

We do the same for the network distance to the respondents with the coldest baseline prorefugee scores (Dist to Coldest), to the treated respondents whose attitudes warmed the most in response to treatment in the short term (Dist to Persuaded), and to the treated respondents whose attitudes cooled the most in response to treatment in the short term (Dist to Backlashed).

Main article · PDF page 14

although networks were measured pretreatment, they might be correlated with unobserved factors that are themselves the true reason that attitudes landed where they did in the endline.

Main article · PDF page 14

Human review: pending

Treatment assignment Partially reported

Households are randomized within each village: 50% in villages 1–2 and approximately 60% in villages 3–4 receive treatment. Control baseline interviews are completed before treated interviews to accommodate anticipated spillovers.

In each of the four study villages, a randomly selected set 7 of households received a perspectivetaking treatment along with a survey to learn beliefs, attitudes, demographics, and social networks. The remaining control households were only surveyed. Treatment and control households were surveyed again approximately 2 weeks later.

Main article · PDF page 5

Fifty percent of households in Villages 1 and 2, about 60% in 3 and 4.

Main article · PDF page 5

Because we were interested in and anticipated spillovers, we completed baseline surveys for the control households first before beginning any treated ones in each village.

Main article · PDF page 5

Main-text details requiring clarification:

  • The random draw procedure and fixed-count versus independent assignment rule require clarification.

Human review: pending

Estimand Partially reported

The article labels the immediate within-treated before–after change an average treatment effect on the treated and reports longer-term changes and network spillover associations. Appendix C supplies an alternative between-arm ATE calculation.

We test Hypothesis 1 by comparing the treated respondents’ pro-refugee score before and after treatment. On average, respondents ’ pro-refugee score increased 2.5 points in immediate response to treatment.

Main article · PDF page 8

We also calculate the Average Treatment Effect (2.9), which requires accounting for some subtle stable unit treatment value assumption (SUTVA) concerns (see Appendix C of the Supplementary Material).

Main article · PDF page 8

The relationship between network neighbors ’ views and endline views is more evidence in support of Hypothesis 2 (the presence of social processing) and persists through a variety of specifications and added demographic and network controls.

Main article · PDF page 14

Main-text details requiring clarification:

  • An explicit potential-outcomes estimand incorporating interview timing and network exposure would clarify the reported contrasts.

Human review: pending

Analysis model Partially reported

The article uses two-sided difference-in-means t-tests for attitude changes and OLS for endline scores with baseline and network variables, treatment interactions and alternative distance measures.

Results of two-sided difference in means t-test indicated by label, comparing baseline 2 to baseline 1 and endline to baseline 1.

Main article · PDF page 9

In Table 5, the relationships between these network features and a respondent ’s endline attitudes toward refugees are shown as coefficients in an OLS regression.

Main article · PDF page 13

three network variables, and their interaction with treatment to account for the possibility that spillovers work differently for treated and control (Vazquez-Bare 2022).

Main article · PDF page 14

  • The regression standard-error estimator and its treatment of network dependence are unspecified in the reviewed passages.

Human review: pending

Implementation and responses Partially reported

Endline retention is 488/521 households. Network regressions use 474 after removing 14 isolates, and distance specifications use 470 after removing four households in disconnected pairs. Early spillovers influence interview-time baseline measurements.

488 of the 521 households remained in our study through the endline. The 6% who dropped out do not systematically differ from those who remained in the study in terms of demographic or network attributes (see Appendix D of the Supplementary Material).

Main article · PDF page 9

This regression drops 14 observations from the 488 who remained in the endline because the network measures can only be calculated for respondents who have at least one network neighbor.

Main article · PDF page 14

Four households are in components of size two in Village 1. They are not connected to the reference households (all in the giant component) by any paths of finite length, so are dropped from these analyses which reduces the sample size by four more.

Main article · PDF page 14

Main-text details requiring clarification:

  • Treatment refusal, fidelity and item-level missingness procedures require clarification.

Human review: pending

Targeted reviews

Focused findings from selected passages. Review scope is recorded for each entry.

P082 · Targeted review

Targeted main-text diagnostic reading, PDF pp.6–7; not a completed eight-field pass or 146 experiment records.

Clustering and analytical response

In 2018, assignment used groups defined by 1–4-minute survey-entry intervals. Standard errors were clustered at the same time-group level. Record the assignment rules separately by year.

Because they wished to have more evenly sized treatment arms, in the 2018 data, treatments were cluster-assigned based on the time at which respondents entered the survey. At the start of each cluster (lasting 1 –4 minutes), the target sample size for each treatment condition was defined as the ideal number of responses to that treatment given the current sample size. The cluster was then assigned to the treatment with the fewest responses relative to its target. To handle this complication, we cluster our standard errors at the level of these clusters in our analysis in the 2018 data.

Main article · PDF page 6

Assignment probabilities

In 2020, individuals were randomly assigned using dynamically updated probabilities that were not retained. The authors discuss covariate and time balance as evidence that the impact may be limited. Complete assignment probabilities remain unavailable.

In the 2020 data, treatments were assigned randomly at the individual level, but using dynamically updating assignment probabilities. For each respondent, each treatment was sampled in proportion to the remaining number of responses it would need before reaching its target. Unfortunately, these assignment probabilities were not saved, but data on covariate balance suggest that there is good balance on both baseline demographic and political covariates, and better-than-expected balance on time, indicating that this assignment scheme functioned essentially as implicitly blocking on time. The consequences of this procedure for sampling variability are, therefore, likely trivial.

Main article · PDF page 6

Mid-study changes to treatment arms

Some experiments added or removed treatment arms during fielding. The authors discard observations collected after these changes and state that this decision was not specified in the pre-analysis plan.

A second complication is that, in a small number of cases (four studies in 2018, seven studies in 2020), Swayable either added or subtracted a treatment at some point during the experiment, then used an algorithm to dynamically update what fraction of subjects were assigned to treatments over time. This procedure generates different probabilities of assignment for different units that are difficult to reconstruct. Our solution is to simply discard any data collected after the moment of addition or subtraction of the new treatment (detected as a treatment with no responses collected in the initial or final 10% of the study). This procedure was not declared in the PAP.

Main article · PDF page 6

Attrition, missing outcomes, and analytical responses

Most surveys lack records for respondents who were assigned but did not complete the survey. Data needed for a full-sample differential-attrition assessment are incomplete. The authors reconstructed records for 22 experiments from 2020 and compared inverse-probability-weighted results. Missing no-opinion responses in 2018 are discussed separately. The robustness check covers those 22 experiments; attrition in the remaining experiments requires further review.

A third complication and limitation of the data arises from the fact that, in most of the surveys, Swayable did not keep records of data for people who were assigned a treatment but did not provide outcome data, so we cannot directly verify whether the treatments induce differential attrition, which would be a threat to inference.

Main article · PDF page 6

Fourth and lastly, in Appendix B.3 of the Supplementary Material, we present an analysis of this subset of studies that compares our unweighted treatment effect estimates to inverseprobability-weighted estimates (using the procedure described in Gerber and Green 2012, chap. 7), finding virtually no differences in estimated effects.

Main article · PDF page 7

In Supplementary Figure OA12, we consider a range of alternative approaches to handle these missing outcomes—including inverse probability weighting, as well as fitting meta-regressions with study-level intercepts— and find that our results remain fairly stable regardless of the choices made.

Main article · PDF page 7

Supplementary-material reviews

Field-by-field supplementary findings and source quotations are grouped below by paper. Comparisons between the main article, appendix and replication materials form a separate analysis.

P008 · Field-by-field supplementary review
P008-E01 · Sample recruitment
After supplementary review

Appendix B describes age, nationality and residence eligibility, IP-geolocation screening, age-by-gender advertisement budgets, and a 100 DZD completion incentive. Initial survey starts and assignment-stage counts require clarification.

On the first page, respondents answered three eligibility questions: age (must be over 18), nationality (must be Algerian), and current location (must be currently in Algeria). We later verified that they were living in Algeria using the geolocation of IP addresses; we exclude any survey completed outside of Algeria.

Appendix · PDF page 11

In the consent form, respondents were informed that if they completed the survey, they would receive 100DZD ( <$1) of mobile phone credit.

Appendix · PDF page 12

We created multiple ads (each with the same picture) and targeted each to a specific age-gender group: Algerian women aged 35-44, for instance. We then altered how much we would spend on each ad each day (the “quota”): we set the minimum, $1/day, for groups over-represented on Facebook, such as men aged 18-24 and 25-34. We spent progressively larger amounts on under-represented groups, up to $10/day on Algerian women over 65 years old.

P008-E01 · Implementation and responses
After supplementary review

Appendix B.6 reports roughly 81% passing in each arm (p=0.8 for each treatment-control comparison) and includes failures in robustness models. Table B.6 uses 1,059 observations. Appendix B.7 lists quota support before the attention check, while the main narrative places the check before the outcome discussion. Table B.3 gives 501 opponents and 410 supporters, compared with 451/386 in the prose and 471/396 in Table 3; sample-flow reconciliation remains open.

About 18.5% of the original sample failed the attention check, which asked what percent of parliamentary lists are reserved for women. The answer had been provided in all treatment groups, including the control. That suggests that those 18.5% did not carefully read, or at least could not recall, the text of the experiment. As a result, they are unlikely to be affected by the experiment, and we thus exclude them from the main results. Removing them does not appear to create differential attrition bias. Figure B.6 shows that all treatment groups were equally likely to pass the attention check.

About 81% in each prime passed, none significantly different from the control (p=0.8 for each).

P010 · Field-by-field supplementary review
P010-E01 · Sample recruitment
After supplementary review

Appendix 1 specifies purposive site selection, ages 20–50, same-gender referrals, household/nuclear-family exclusions, telephone recruitment from randomized lists, a referral-eligibility criterion in both arms, and $20 compensation.

Recruitment for the community meetings was based on a random sample of UNHCR registration records, drawn from all registered refugees living in the research sites between the ages of 20–50.

Appendix · PDF page 5

Recruiters were provided with four lists of names from UNHCR data for each site, one per gender per experimental group. Within each gender, selection into one group or another was randomized and the order of names in each contact list was randomized. Recruiters were instructed to contact people by phone in the order listed until recruiting a sufficient number of participants.

Appendix · PDF page 5

These three referrals needed to be between the ages of 20 and 50, live in the same town as the referrer, be the same gender as the referrer, and not be members of the referrer’s nuclear family or household.

PDF page 4 · PDF page 5 · PDF page 6

P010-E01 · Treatments
After supplementary review

Referral questions asked about the three Syrians contacted most during the preceding two weeks, including electronic communication. Four vignettes covered aid distribution, a shop dispute, checkpoints, and a fight; vignette order was randomized.

In gathering referrals for the networked community meetings, people were requested to give the names of the three Syrians in their community who they had interacted with most over the past two weeks, where interaction was explained to include face-to-face communication and electronic communication via telephone, texting, WhatsApp, Facebook, etc.

Appendix · PDF page 6

The order of the audio vignettes was randomized to eliminate order effects.

P010-E01 · Outcomes
After supplementary review

Appendix 1.11 documents codebook development, randomized transcript order and blinded coding; Section 12 supplies vignette text. The full coding guide and supplementary tables are separately linked resources.

The researchers who developed the coding guide and I were blind to treatment status while developing the guide. In my coding of transcripts, I randomized the order in which I read the groups and vignettes within each group.

Appendix · PDF page 10

PDF page 9 · PDF page 10 · PDF page 11 · PDF page 25 · PDF page 26

P010-E01 · Pretreatment covariates
After supplementary review

Appendix 3 tests balance among randomly sampled participants. Appendix 8 adds moderator and country indicators and treatment interactions with demeaned covariates. The questionnaire records attributes after the meeting.

I run a test of joint balance (aka, joint orthogonality), testing the joint hypothesis:β1 = β2 = . . . = βk = 0, by running an F-test on a linear regression of treatment assignment on measured covariates, subsetted to randomly sampled participants.

Appendix · PDF page 13

I present the coefficients estimates from regressions including covariates including an indicator variable for moderator and an indicator variable for whether a community meeting took place in Jordan.

Appendix · PDF page 17

PDF page 13 · PDF page 16 · PDF page 17 · PDF page 18

P010-E01 · Treatment assignment
After supplementary review

Appendix 7 specifies one treated and one control meeting per site-gender block. Figure 1A labels group probabilities as 1/2 and individual probabilities as 1/10 versus 9/10. These individual probabilities require reconciliation with the main-text description of 308 census recruits (28 seeds plus 280 control participants) and the recruitment overbooking described in Appendix 1.6.

I blocked treatment assignment on site and gender, with two community meetings in each block, and randomly assigned one community meeting to control and one to treatment in each site-gender block.

P(Zi=0)=9/10P(Zi=1)=1/10

Within each gender, selection into one group or another was randomized and the order of names in each contact list was randomized.

P010-E01 · Estimand
After supplementary review

Appendix 2 formalizes group and individual potential outcomes, assignment assumptions, and sample-analogue estimation. Both contrasts are retained within one linked study unit, with distinct analysis populations.

Randomly assigning an individual to be the first member of a group that is then recruited through referral sampling or random sampling also implicitly randomly assigns the group to its treatment status.

Appendix · PDF page 12

I use the plug-in principle to estimate the inferential targets, the left-hand side of Equations 7, 8, and 9, with the sample analogues of the right-hand side of the equations.

Appendix · PDF page 12

PDF page 12 · PDF page 13

P010-E01 · Analysis model
After supplementary review

Appendix 7 confirms paired assignment within site-gender blocks. Appendix 8 describes OLS and Lin-style covariate adjustment with meeting-clustered standard errors and 95% confidence intervals. Additional regression tables are linked through the replication materials.

Therefore, I conduct randomization inference by simulating 100,000 treatment assignment vectors under the blocking scheme. For each vector of simulated treatment labels I calculate a test statistic according to the simulated treatment assignment vector.

Appendix · PDF page 17

I calculate the results in Figures 3Aa and 3Ab using OLS regression with robust standard errors, clustered at the community meeting level. I present treatment effect point estimates and 95% clusterrobust confidence intervals for all the outcomes presented in the main paper.

P010-E01 · Implementation and responses
After supplementary review

Appendix 1.6 reports a recruitment error: control recruiters contacted 11 people in Lebanon and 12 in Jordan versus a target of 13 in the networked arm. Average group sizes were 8.2 versus 9.3. Appendix 1.8 describes manual moderator allocation. Appendix 4.2 uses 227 individuals for a diversity check versus 258 in the main individual analyses; the missingness rule remains unclear.

Although attendance rates across treatment and control arms are balanced, due to an error in recruitment, unnetworked groups were one person smaller on average than networked groups.

Appendix · PDF page 7

Recruiters in Lebanon contacted only 11 people to fill 10 spots in the unnetworked groups, and recruiters in Jordan contacted 12 people to fill 10 spots in the unnetworked groups. This should have been held constant at 13 to match the number of people contacted for the networked group in each country.

8.2 vs. 9.3 overall, 8.7 vs. 9.6 in Lebanon, and 7.6 vs. 8.9 in Jordan.

the uncertainty is greater at the group level, due in part to small sample size (56), compared to the individual level (227).

Appendix · PDF page 19

PDF page 19

P027-E04 · Estimand
After supplementary review

Primarily-male versus primarily-female vignette effects on three normalized outcomes.

We tested all hypotheses related to Study 2 and 3 in exactly the same way as in th e corresponding original US studies. In summary, we test if respondents under-estimate the victimization of men in war (H1), the effect of describing the victims in the vignette as “primarily men” as opposed to “primarily women” on perceived innocence, aid support, and refugee acceptance ( H2b, H3b, H4b), whether groups of immigrants with a high share of men are less likely to be accepted ( H5a), and whether information about men’s vulnerability in war (same information treatment as in the US study) mitigates anti-male bias in refugee acceptance ( H5b) and increases support for aid that specifically addresses the needs of men ( H5c).

P027-E05 · Estimand
After supplementary review

Profile-attribute effects, majority-male × information interaction, and information effect on aid support.

We tested all hypotheses related to Study 2 and 3 in exactly the same way as in th e corresponding original US studies. In summary, we test if respondents under-estimate the victimization of men in war (H1), the effect of describing the victims in the vignette as “primarily men” as opposed to “primarily women” on perceived innocence, aid support, and refugee acceptance ( H2b, H3b, H4b), whether groups of immigrants with a high share of men are less likely to be accepted ( H5a), and whether information about men’s vulnerability in war (same information treatment as in the US study) mitigates anti-male bias in refugee acceptance ( H5b) and increases support for aid that specifically addresses the needs of men ( H5c).

P044 · Field-by-field supplementary review
P044-E01 · Sample recruitment
After supplementary review

Appendix A details village selection within 3 km, largest-village prioritization and household eligibility. Consent, third-grade Hindi comprehension and an attendance commitment were required; failing households were replaced before assignment.

Enumerators visited each house in person for the baseline survey, which included three additional eligibility criteria. First, enumerators obtained official oral consent from both children as well as one parent or guardian present to conduct the baseline as well as return for an endline survey after the classroom sessions. Second, we included a one-item measure of students’ basic (third-grade) reading comprehension in Hindi. If students failed this item, the household in question was replaced in our sampling frame. Third, we asked both students and parents to verbally affirm that the student would be able to attend four classroom sessions during the stipulated weeks and timings; if either said no we dropped the household from the sample and replaced it.

1. Standardize each true and false statement with respect to the control group; 2. Calculate the ICW index of true information and the ICW index of false information; 3. Standardize both ICW subindices with respect to the control group 4. Calculate the difference between the ICW T RU E and ICW F ALSE indices to get a measure of discernment; and lastly, 5. Standardize the resulting discernment measure relative to the control group so that any treatment effects are in terms of standard deviations of the final discernment measure relative to the control group.

Appendix · PDF page 46

PDF page 45 · PDF page 46 · PDF page 47

P044-E01 · Treatment assignment
After supplementary review

Appendix A.2 reports 294 treated/289 control villages. Even strata use complete half allocation; odd strata receive special handling. In 25 libraries, one village from each spillover stratum forms a randomized mixed pair. Post-assignment spillover classification is used for heterogeneity; original strata are used for main-model fixed effects.

In cases where there was an even number of villages within one library-spillover stratum, we used complete randomization to assign exactly half of the villages to treatment and half to control. For example, in a given library area if there were 4 low-spillover villages, we assigned 2 to treatment and 2 to control using complete randomization. In case of an uneven number of villages within a spillover stratum, the last village was randomly assigned to treatment or control. Due to this, we end up with 5 libraries where the number of treatment villages is either one more ore one less than the number of control villages. In libraries where there was an uneven number of villages within both spillover strata, high and low (this was the case for 25 libraries), we randomly selected one village from the low-spillover stratum and one from the high-spillover stratum and then again used complete randomization to assign one of them to treatment and one of them to control. For example, in libraries with 3 low-spillover and 3 high-spillover villages, we randomly selected one low and one high-spillover village, and then used complete randomization to assign one of these to treatment and one to control. With the remainder (4, two in each stratum), we again use complete randomization by stratum. Hence, in the 25 libraries where this was the case, we technically had a third, ‘mixed’ randomization stratum that consisted of one low-spillover village and one high-spillover village. Since the exact villages that were part of this third stratum were randomly drawn, we had theoretical reasons to expect the potential outcomes to be more correlated with other villages within the same spillover category, rather than between the two villages that were randomly selected to be part of the mixed randomization stratum.

Appendix · PDF page 4

Overall, these procedures lead us to assign roughly the same number of units to treatment (294) and control (289).

Appendix · PDF page 32

First, as described in the randomization procedure in Appendix A, we randomized to treatment and control within library-spillover strata. Consequently, our main baseline models in the paper include fixed effects (FEs) for library-spillover strata. However, in our PAP, we mistakenly stated that the main models would include district-level FEs alone. In practice, districts were not part of the randomization procedure. Instead, we used the library-spillover strata to ensure balance on key characteristics defining these strata (e.g., spillover potential, library attributes, proximity to other villages, and development indicators). Therefore, the appropriate specification includes FEs by library-spillover strata. In the PAP, we had also indicated that we would estimate models separately with stratum FEs and library FEs. Instead, our main specification combines stratum and library FEs into a single set of library-spillover strata FEs. Nevertheless, we report a number of alternate specifications in Appendix J, including the original PAP model with district FE.

P067-E01 · Implementation and responses
After supplementary review

Differential-response tests and exposure limitations recorded. Rmd analyses omit missing outcomes; causal assumptions and CACE implementation require human review.

The Survey Attrition section on appendix PDF page 9 reports a small, marginally significant response difference. Table OA5 gives a MariCruz/Kandy versus LGBTQ difference of approximately −0.01, p=0.04.

Another design assumption is that the treatment does not affect the composition of the individuals who take each follow-up survey (Broockman, Kalla, and Sekhon 2017). We investigate this by regressing an indicator for responding to a post-treatment survey on indicators of treatment assignment. In the post-treatment survey, we find substantively small but borderline significant evidence of differential attrition. For this regression, the base condition is the LGBTQ ad.

Appendix · PDF page 9

The appendix regresses response on treatment, baseline covariates, and interactions. The robust joint F test for interactions has p=0.4. Further assessment of attrition bias should consider response differences and the relevant assumptions.

To test whether attrition patterns are similar by covariates in treatment and placebo, we use a linear regression of whether or not an individual responded to the follow-up survey on treatment, baseline covariates, and treatment-covariate interactions. We then perform a heteroskedasticityrobust F-test of the hypothesis that all the interaction coefficients are zero. This procedure is standard practice (Gerber and Green 2012). The p-value on this F-test is 0.4, suggesting there does not appear to be evidence of asymmetrical attrition.

P075 · Field-by-field supplementary review
P075-E01 · Sample recruitment
After supplementary review

Appendix A.1 traces 240 candidate centers to 162 verified and 149 eligible. A.4 samples blocks, households and one adult per household, and specifies the leadership quotas.

To sample residents, survey enumerators first created a map of all blocks ( manzanas) in each community; when available, these maps were cross-checked against satellite images. Enumerators then randomly selected three blocks per community. Since most blocks consist of only a few households, in most cases enumerators surveyed all households on the selected blocks. If the total number of households on the selected blocks resulted in fewer than 18 respondents, enumerators randomly selected a fourth block to survey. If the total number of households resulted in more than 18 respondents, enumerators randomly selected households on each block, with the number of households selected proportional to the number of households on the block. Enumerators then randomly selected one adult resident (18+ years of age) from each selected household.

Importantly, there is no overlap between these two groups: either Police Inspectors or police officers participated in Module 1 in all municipalities.

P075-E01 · Treatment assignment

A.3.1 enumerates 28 blocks: Oriente sizes 7,7,7,6; Nordeste four of 6; Ariari seven of 4 and three of 5; Valle five of 5 and five of 6. blockTools formed blocks, with remainder communities reassigned by similar mean population.

In Oriente Antioque ̃no, we created three blocks of seven communities and one block of six (27 communities total). In Nordeste Antioque ̃no, we created four blocks of six communities (24 communities total). P075-E02 · Treatments
After supplementary review

Control respondents count four possible dispute-resolution mechanisms; treated respondents receive a fifth item, reporting to an armed group.

The control group list included four items: (1) report to the JAC; (2) write a letter to the mayor; (3) resolve the problem directly; or (4) report to an indigenous leader. Because our sample did not include any indigenous communities, we assumed that respondents would be very unlikely to select the fourth item, thus reducing the risk of floor effects. The treatment group list included an additional sensitive item: (5) report to an armed group.

Respondents were read a list of mechanisms that communities might use to resolve “basic problems of coexistence (convivencia),” then asked to count how many of those mechanisms were in use in their own communities.

Appendix · PDF page 13

PDF page 13

P091-E01 · Treatments
After supplementary review

Appendix A supplies the five-step protocol and examples: rapport, refugee narratives, compassion, concerns and advocacy. Local Lugbara-speaking interviewers practiced the intervention.

Following Broockman and Kalla (2016), our intervention proceeded in several steps and allowed for some flexibility to allow for a natural exchange rather than a fully scripted one. We trained the survey team to carry out the intervention with great care, to ensure that they thoroughly understood each component and had ample opportunity to practice the intervention. All research team members who conducted interviews were from West Nile (but not the villages we studied) and spoke the local language (Lugbara).

Appendix · PDF page 2

PDF page 2 · PDF page 3 · PDF page 4

P091-E01 · Outcomes
After supplementary review

Appendix C tabulates both arms. Appendix E.2 defines shortest-path distances to village-specific extreme-attitude reference sets, using at least three households with ties included.

Our network distance measures are calculated as the length of the shortest path in the village network between each household and a reference set of households. We use four reference sets: the households of the respondents with the warmest baseline attitudes, the coldest baseline attitudes, the largest positive change in response to treatment, and the largest backlash to treatment measured as having the coldest second baseline score after treatment among those who reacted negatively to treatment. Each village has its own reference sets. For each village and each reference set, we selected the cutoff value of the relevant variable such that at least three villagers were included. Then we accepted into the set all villagers with a value that extreme, which often resulted in more than three in the top set due to ties.

Appendix · PDF page 17

PDF page 11 · PDF page 17

P091-E01 · Pretreatment covariates
After supplementary review

Appendix E.3 summarizes baseline/network variables and checks alternative model specifications. Demographic characteristics used in attrition comparisons include age, religion, occupation, education and residence duration.

Table 9: Summaries of main variables in regression analyses. Mean values of: treatment status; a count of the number of treated neighbors; an indicator for having at least one treated neighbor; baseline pro-refugee score; mean neighborhood pro-refugee scores; network distance to one of the warmest households at baseline, one of the coldest households at baseline, one of the treated households most persuaded by treatment, andone of the treated households that responded most negatively to treatment. Also displays count of the reference households to which distance variables are calculated in the village, as well as the number of households in baseline and endline.

Appendix · PDF page 18

PDF page 13 · PDF page 18

P091-E01 · Treatment assignment
After supplementary review

Appendix C.1 confirms household randomization and the control-first sequence. It documents rising baseline attitudes among treated households interviewed later in the treatment wave.

We anticipated spillovers from treatment, and so our design randomized households into treatment and control but then completed all control baseline surveys first within each village. Consequently, we have better balance on baseline scores between control and treated if we compare all control to people treated early in the treatment wave, for instance on the first day of the treatment wave (Treated Day 1).

Appendix · PDF page 11

PDF page 11 · PDF page 12

P091-E01 · Estimand
After supplementary review

Appendix C.1 compares treated post-intervention scores with control baseline scores; its corrected comparison restricts treated respondents to the first treatment day (54 treated and 232 controls), addressing early spillovers.

The corrected ATE uses only respondents who were surveyed up to and including the first day in which treatment was administered. This includes all control respondents and the treated whose survey occurred on the first day of the treatment wave. This set of treated should be least susceptible to spillovers raising their baseline scores.

Villagers who are in their own component (only relevant for villages 1 and 2) are dropped because their paths to any member of the reference set are infinitely long.

Appendix · PDF page 19

Note that the number of exposures was not randomly assigned, so this dose-response analysis is observational, rather than causal (i.e., we can only estimate the effect for people who received different doses, but the number of doses itself is not randomly assigned).

Third, using survey experimental data reported by Winters and Weitz-Shapiro ( 2016) and Weitz-Shapiro and Winters ( 2016), I estimate how voter updating varies in bureaucratic quality. P082 · Hewitt et al. - 2024 - How Experiments Help Campaigns Persuade Voters Evidence from a Large Archive of Campaigns’ Own Expe Included for diagnosis

Include the experimental archive. The paper reports individual random assignment in 2020; time-group assignment and standard errors in 2018 require separate coding. Individual extraction of the 146 experiments remains pending.

Because they wished to have more evenly sized treatment arms, in the 2018 data, treatments were cluster-assigned based on the time at which respondents entered the survey. At the start of each cluster (lasting 1 –4 minutes), the target sample size for each treatment condition was defined as the ideal number of responses to that treatment given the current sample size. The cluster was then assigned to the treatment with the fewest responses relative to its target. To handle this complication, we cluster our standard errors at the level of these clusters in our analysis in the 2018 data.

Main article · PDF page 6

In the 2020 data, treatments were assigned randomly at the individual level, but using dynamically updating assignment probabilities.

Main article · PDF page 6

A third complication and limitation of the data arises from the fact that, in most of the surveys, Swayable did not keep records of data for people who were assigned a treatment but did not provide outcome data, so we cannot directly verify whether the treatments induce differential attrition, which would be a threat to inference.
